The Rush-Job Presidency
2026-09-05

The Rush-Job Presidency

The Atlantic | 2026-09-05

Visit Original Website
Construction is notoriously slow-going work. But in Donald Trump’s Washington, cranes and bulldozers appear like DoorDash deliveries: fast, frictionless, and on demand. To build the president’s White House ballroom, roughly 250 workers have been pouring concrete and installing steel framework, keeping the site humming for 20 hours a day, seven days a week. The National Park Service has proposed a similarly intensive schedule for Trump’s planned triumphal arch: Construction will begin, Interior Secretary Doug Burgum announced on Thursday, in the next two weeks. And officials are trying to shut down the Kennedy Center for two years to carry out renovations that consultants say will take twice as long if the building remains open during the work. These are very different and ambitious projects, but they share the same presumed deadline: January 20, 2029. They also share a risk: The compressed schedules could lead to shoddy work that will require more repairs later. Trump has less than three years to complete his transformation of Washington, D.C., before his projects fall into—and perhaps die in—the hands of a successor. “Nobody will do it once I’m gone. When I leave here, nobody will,” he told New York magazine. The next president, whomever they are, likely won’t share Trump’s enthusiasm for gold finishes and Italian marble. To get the work done, his administration has found ways to circumvent the procedural obstacles that tend to slow federal projects. Construction—one of the world’s least productive industries—may present its own paradox. Research has shown that long hours, additional shifts, and overlapping work tasks can shorten a project’s timeline—but only to a point. In many cases, the effort to build faster can invite new delays, higher costs, and revisions. Trump’s botched repairs to the Reflecting Pool ahead of the nation’s 250th-birthday celebrations, in July, offer a classic example of the problem. The ballroom project, a hulking party venue that overshadows the White House’s main building and its modest neoclassical design, could be another. The Supreme Court ruled 5–4 this week that its construction could continue, saying that the preservationists trying to halt the project did not have standing to sue. The three liberal dissenting justices—along with, notably, Chief Justice John G. Roberts—argued that the construction was “likely unlawful.” But government filings claim that it’s already 65 percent complete—even as project leaders have continually revised design plans. By the time a new lawsuit from a challenger with stronger standing advances through the courts, how far along will the ballroom be? Read: The Court’s ballroom decision is a sign of deeper problems “We have hundreds of happy construction workers in D.C. busily doing their job building the finest Ballroom/Military complex anywhere in the World,” Trump wrote on Truth Social this week after the Supreme Court decision. With his lawyers saying that the project will be “substantially” finished by November, the clock is ticking fast. Trump’s building spree presents a tricky calculation: The returns he wants are about time rather than cost. But speed in construction can get expensive fast, especially for a project whose anticipated costs have been hard to nail down. When Trump announced the ballroom plans in July 2025, he put the price at roughly $200 million, to be paid for by private donors. By October, he’d knocked down the East Wing of the White House without seeking any approvals. As the work has started, stopped (because of the preservationists’ lawsuit), and started again, the original estimate has tripled to $600 million, and taxpayers are expected to cover half of that amount, according to internal documents from the ballroom’s general contractor, Clark Construction, that were obtained by The Washington Post. Those projections could rise again. “Accelerated construction is always at higher cost, no matter what you do,” Awad Hanna, the chair of the construction-engineering and -management program at the University of Wisconsin at Madison, told me. There are essentially three ways to make construction move faster, according to Hanna: Workers can put in overtime, contractors can add second or third shifts, or the project can resort to overmanning—that is, adding an unusually large number of workers to a site. Each method may buy some time but sacrifices some productivity, Hanna said. Researchers have documented that trade-off for decades. A 1997 study published by the American Society of Civil Engineers examined 121 weeks of labor-productivity data from four industrial projects and found efficiency losses of roughly 10 to 15 percent for 50-to-60-hour workweeks. Hanna’s own research has found similar patterns. With overmanning, the problem is that there’s only so much room for specific tasks to be performed. As the site fills up with workers, they can find themselves competing for tools, materials, and information—or simply getting in one another’s way. Meanwhile, overtime can lead to fatigue for workers who are spending 10 to 12 hours on their feet, carrying heavy objects while also needing to remain diligent about their own safety. There is another way to speed up construction without adding hours or workers: starting construction before a design is complete. The industry calls this fast-tracking. But a 2023 study found that construction professionals perceived the fast-track strategy as posing a different set of risks, including construction errors and productivity declines, when better productivity is supposed to be the whole point. A certain amount of secrecy surrounds the White House worksite, in part because of a large military structure that is being built underground, beneath where the ballroom will stand. The methods to speed up the work are unclear, although the teams behind the project are reputable: Clark Construction is a seasoned federal contractor, and the architectural firm Shalom Baranes Associates repaired the Pentagon after 9/11. A White House spokesperson told me that the project is proceeding with the 20-hour schedule for now. In April, Trump turned his focus to the Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool, a century-old landmark that has long struggled with leaks and algae blooms. Trump first claimed that he could complete a repair project in a week for less than $2 million. “I have a guy who’s unbelievable at doing swimming pools,” he told reporters at the time. The costs climbed to more than $16 million, which included the installation of a waterproof liner tinted in a color that he called American-flag blue. But shortly after the pool reopened, in June, algae returned in force and the sealant peeled, leading crews to repeatedly drain and scrub the basin over the summer. Now it sits completely empty and fenced off. Trump accused vandals of damaging the pool, but his own U.S. attorney for D.C. refuted those claims, pointing to the contractor’s failures and the Interior Department providing “less than fulsome information at the outset of the case.” Federal officials recently hired a new contractor to deal with the algae. Trump has already cleared one obstacle blocking the ballroom. But the legal case is not over. Following the emergency ruling, the government still has to formally ask the Supreme Court to hear its appeal. And the National Trust for Historic Preservation, which brought the suit against Trump, can continue a fight in the lower courts. For each of Trump’s Washington projects, officials have beefed up their justifications in one way or another in the face of legal challenges. In the administration’s view: The Kennedy Center may have to be “taken down” if its planned two-year shutdown for renovations is blocked. (Critics have pushed back on officials’ claims that the building’s conditions are existentially dire.) The Reflecting Pool’s needed repairs were urgent enough to exempt them from the typical competitive-bidding process. (The urgency was the July 4 celebrations.) And now? The White House ballroom is needed for security reasons, because it’s connected to a military bunker. Read: The Kennedy Center has a problem that courts can’t fix The administration has also pointed to the ballroom’s progress in arguing against an injunction, raising a larger question about whether speed itself has become a form of power in Trump’s building spree. If a president can build faster than Congress or regulators can review, legal constraints may matter less in practice—not because they have disappeared but because enforcing them comes too late. This week’s Supreme Court ruling has already prompted at least one federal judge to consider its implications for another Trump project. Yesterday, U.S. District Judge Carl J. Nichols ordered lawyers representing the Interior Department to address how the Court’s ballroom decision affects its pending motion to dismiss a lawsuit challenging work on the Reflecting Pool. When asked about the start of excavations for the triumphal arch, the White House spokesperson said in an email, “Work will begin that does not require approval, but rather helps in the process. We will of course continue on our path of seeking final approval from all applicable entities.” The arch still needs final approval from the National Capital Planning Commission, to which Trump has added loyalists. But the administration has also been working to water down historic-preservation rules governing federal projects just as the arch has become subject to that process. Meanwhile, the Kennedy Center—which Trump also controls—cannot start its renovation until a judge signs off on the latest plan. But this week, the center removed a prominent artwork near the Potomac River, a sign that top-down change has begun. All three projects—the ballroom, the arch, and the Kennedy Center—may soon be moving forward simultaneously, red tape cleared (or ignored). But sped-up construction can impose its own obstacles. When unexpected delays crop up, even presidents have to wait.
The Atlantic
Don’t Ban AI on Campus
2026-09-05

Don’t Ban AI on Campus

The Atlantic | 2026-09-05

Visit Original Website
Students returning to campus this semester are arriving with a great deal of anxiety about AI and what it will mean for their lives after college. They are right to be worried. New research from Stanford shows that, even though the much-hyped AI job apocalypse has yet to materialize, the technology has already caused a nearly 20 percent drop in employment for young workers in AI-exposed occupations, such as computer programming and customer-service work. Anxiety over the looming threat of AI may seem novel, but as a cognitive scientist who has long studied how fear affects learning and performance, I am all too familiar with this kind of unease. This is how I know it is also curable. Several years ago, my former graduate student Ian Lyons and I invited people who were anxious about math to have their brains scanned while they took a math test. We were surprised to find that it wasn’t the test itself that sent people’s brains into a panic. Rather, the neural signals associated with threat detection and pain were most acute when people were merely anticipating the test. We are all now living through AI’s waiting-for-the-math-test phase. AI is transforming classrooms, workplaces, and hiring needs—and experts say more turbulence is coming. Teachers and students are struggling to parse the difference between deploying AI effectively and exploiting the technology to cheat. But rather than tackling this disruptive change and the resulting anxiety head-on, many educators are trying to make AI simply go away. The University of Chicago’s social-science faculty have prohibited most technology and banned the use of AI tools from their core classes. Although law firms are beginning to embrace the benefits of generative AI, the technology apparently makes many law schools squeamish. In May, the UC Berkeley School of Law announced that students are prohibited from using AI in “conceptualizing, outlining, drafting, revising, translating, or editing any work submitted for credit.” The University of Chicago Law School has decided to ban all screens from first-year classrooms entirely. In an atmosphere of uncertainty—and given prevailing concerns about students using AI to cheat—such bans would seem to make some sense. But draconian mandates and screenless classes can’t be the only ways that colleges deal with AI. If universities hope to remain relevant in a world shaped by artificial intelligence, they should strive to guide students to understand and use these tools productively. In my research of math anxiety, we found that the best cure was practicing math. This points to a way forward. When we supply students with the space to thoughtfully explore and engage with these tools, we can help ensure that they graduate into an AI-enabled workforce with more confidence and competence than fear, and with an appreciation for how to use AI tools ethically. But if higher education opts for prohibition over practice, we will squander the chance to help shape how this technology gets used in the world—ensuring that the private sector will do this for us instead. Colleges must start by being clear about what a degree is actually worth in the AI age. At Dartmouth, the Ivy League university where I am president, we believe it’s our job to teach students how to do what AI can’t: decide which questions to ask, exercise sound judgment, navigate ethical complexity, reckon with uncertainty, and take responsibility for their decisions. Then, we have to give them the chance to practice these skills in concert with AI. Harnessing AI ethically and productively requires understanding what the technology is and where it falls short. This is why Dartmouth, along with other schools such as Johns Hopkins University and Rollins College, has employed a critical AI librarian, an educator who helps students and faculty interrogate these models, recognize their biases, and spot unsupported claims. At our DALI Lab, where students design and create technological products, they can now build and stress-test their own AI models, which elevates their understanding of the risks and rewards at play. Our ambition at Dartmouth is for every undergraduate to experience a team-based AI project that relates to their coursework by the time they graduate. Practice using AI matters even more when students start to specialize. To help prepare business students for AI use in the real world, Dartmouth’s Tuck School of Business now requires that they use the technology to build and test solutions for clients in their First-Year Project. Thanks to this experience, students who lacked AI expertise when they entered the program reported greater confidence using the technology by the end of their first year. At Dartmouth’s Geisel School of Medicine, our faculty developed an AI Patient Actor to help medical-school students sharpen their communication and interpersonal skills with the benefit of real-time feedback. The tool is now being used by more than 100 medical institutions worldwide. Walt Hunter: Bring back the blue-book exam Adapting the college experience for the AI era will be an ongoing process, and there is much work to do. But these examples demonstrate what’s both possible and necessary in higher education. Our job as educators is to teach students how to approach AI critically, help them see what AI can’t answer, and practice using these tools effectively. When these students graduate and are out in the world, employers will certainly expect that they have these skills already. Globally, jobs that require advanced AI skills are growing eight times faster than the job market overall, according to an analysis from the professional-services network PwC. At the same time, entry-level roles in AI-exposed fields are more than seven times likelier than other entry-level jobs to require the human skills once reserved for senior executives, including discernment, creativity, and leadership. Never before has the ability to harmonize critical thinking and technology mattered more for graduates. There will not—and should not—be a one-size-fits-all solution to the challenges of supplying this education. One professor may decide that students should write their essays in a blue book in class, while another may ask them to use an AI tool and audit every step of this work. Both choices will serve students when faculty are clear about their ambitions and standards. But outright bans or vague curbs afford no opportunities for students to better understand how to work ethically and effectively with AI. Seventy years ago, pioneering computer scientists launched the field of artificial intelligence on Dartmouth’s campus. That history gives our institution some responsibility for what comes next—not just to prepare students for a future defined by AI, but to give them the tools to help shape that future themselves. If colleges and universities sit out this revolution, we abdicate a sacred commitment that has guided American higher education for centuries: to prepare the next generation of leaders to navigate an ever-changing, always complicated, and frequently perilous world. In a world that promises to be defined by AI, universities that fail to produce graduates who can use it productively will consign themselves to irrelevance.

Crypto Money Still Matters for the Midterms
2026-09-04

Crypto Money Still Matters for the Midterms

The Atlantic | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of The Atlantic Daily, a newsletter that guides you through the biggest stories of the day, helps you discover new ideas, and recommends the best in culture. Sign up for it here. The crypto industry has always moved in cycles: booms and busts; bull markets and harsh, unforgiving “winters.” We’re in something of a lull right now: Bitcoin, the original cryptocurrency, is worth about two-thirds of what it was last fall. Meanwhile, investors are turning their attention to flashier innovations in artificial intelligence. About half of Americans now use AI chatbots, whereas just about a fifth of the country has ever used or invested in crypto. But the crypto industry isn’t dead—as of today, the combined value of all digital currencies is still more than $2.7 trillion—and neither are its political ambitions. Crypto companies have so far put $206 million toward this year’s midterm elections, more than any other corporate sector, including the AI industry, according to a Public Citizen analysis of OpenSecrets and Federal Election Commission data. Fairshake, the industry’s foremost super PAC, has a giant war chest and a reputation for backing winners from both parties (Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer has reportedly warned of the risks of crossing it). Crypto is much further from the spotlight now than it was during its 2024 election-spending spree; back then, the industry was flush with cash, but its reputation was still recovering from the crash of two years prior and from the spectacle of Sam Bankman-Fried’s fraud trial. Even as Americans continue to distrust crypto, they’re now reserving their outrage for other corners of the tech industry, such as data centers and surveillance cameras. Although crypto is no longer as central to the public conversation, it’s still finding ways to flex its power. Fairshake first established itself as a major force during the 2024 election cycle, spending about $133 million in support of its chosen candidates. It started 2026 with a reported $193 million in the bank and has so far spent close to $70 million elevating politicians with an industry-friendly agenda. Fairshake’s record this year has been incredibly strong. Despite a few fairly significant losses, 50 of the 54 candidates it backed ended up winning their race. Not all of them won entirely because of Fairshake’s intervention: Some were incumbents who didn’t have a serious challenger. But an endorsement from Fairshake and its affiliate PACs tends to come with major financial support. The group spent more than $12 million to get a pro-crypto Republican into the general election for Alabama’s open Senate seat—it was “by far the biggest spender” in the primary, per The New York Times. And Protect Progress, Fairshake’s Democrat-focused PAC, spent more than $5 million to make another pro-crypto politician the party’s nominee for the House in Maryland’s Fifth District. Some candidates have incorporated these extreme interventions into their campaign strategy, accusing crypto-backed opponents of being in the industry’s pocket. Minnesota’s Peggy Flanagan, who won the state’s Democratic primary for the Senate, bashed her opponent for being pro-crypto and for being “funded by crypto and AIPAC and health-insurance companies.” In Florida’s Twenty-Fourth District, Fairshake spent more than $2 million trying to defeat Oliver Gilbert’s bid for the House, but Gilbert won his primary anyway. “Trump and his crypto con-artist billionaires just put a target on my back,” he said in one ad. Bloomberg reports that political candidates have purchased more ads mentioning the word crypto this year than they have in any of the past five years, and that the majority of these candidates are anti-crypto. Voters have never been all that passionate about crypto, and the Americans who do care about the tech are still disinclined to trust it. But the country’s attention has been elsewhere lately. Three-quarters of Americans say they’d oppose the construction of a data center in their community. Local governments across the country are now canceling contracts for Flock’s AI-enabled license-plate readers over rising fears of improper surveillance. In a bipartisan effort, 47 states, plus Washington, D.C., and U.S. territories, recently took Meta to trial for allegedly damaging “the mental and physical wellbeing of America’s youth” with its social-media platforms (the company just settled and will pay up to $17.1 billion). Although Fairshake’s spending spree has drawn attention to the role of crypto in politics, there’s been no equivalent public movement this year against the sector. When Politico asked Cynthia Lummis, arguably the country’s most pro-crypto senator, whether voters care about crypto, she replied, “No.” To get a sense of how much the industry’s public profile has shifted, it’s useful to think back on the past six years. Permit me a short history lesson. During the coronavirus pandemic, a speculative bubble massively boosted the industry’s profile. Then, in 2022, everything melted down. Bitcoin lost more than 60 percent of its value, and a series of high-profile corporate implosions created chaos throughout the sector. Executives from several major crypto companies were charged with fraud, but it was FTX’s Bankman-Fried who became, at least for a while, the industry’s criminal mascot. Last year, crypto ended up with a new mascot in the form of Donald Trump. Once in office, he threw his support behind the industry, implementing a deregulatory agenda and signing a major crypto bill into law. As cryptocurrencies soared, he also personally made $1.4 billion from his family’s businesses in the sector, leading to widespread allegations of corruption. As digital currencies have come back down to earth, they have started to feel more mundane. Crypto persists in partnerships with major financial institutions, in exchange-trading funds, and in the payment apps you may already have on your phone. None of this is particularly exciting—we’re unlikely to see an NFT at the Met Gala next year, as we did in 2021, or at the upcoming MTV Video Music Awards, as we did in 2022—yet it’s still a massive industry. Without that same level of visibility, crypto interests aren’t necessarily a public “target the way they were with the fallout of FTX, but on the other hand, they’ve had to struggle to stay relevant,” Mark Hays, an associate director at Americans for Financial Reform (a crypto-critical watchdog), told me; one of their strategies is to “continue to spend heavily to show that they still have clout.” The industry has plenty of battles left to fight, though—and plenty of cash to put toward winning them. Here are four new stories from The Atlantic: You have, by now, probably come across the notion of maxxing—looks-maxxing, sleep-maxxing, protein-maxxing, potassium-maxxing, sun-maxxing, testosterone-maxxing. Etymologically speaking, maxxing seems to be a descendant of somewhat recent slang—for those of us who remember the late 20th century, “maxin’ and relaxin’ ” and “to the max” both come to mind—but in practice, maxxing is a reductionist worldview cloaked in expansionist language. The term is often invoked to describe a narrowly focused goal: If you’re maxxing, you’re likely trying to make yourself smaller, sharper, more chiseled. Maxxing requires sacrifice. It requires careful measurement. And in this way, maxxing is a consequence of our broader optimization-obsessed culture, a fixation fueled by every product, device, app, and service that trains people to collect and analyze data generated by their own behavior. Mark your calendars. The Permanent Questions (launching September 13) is a new podcast featuring the Atlantic staff writer David Brooks in conversation with the world’s deepest thinkers. What Was in the Mind of the Holdout Juror in the Lindsay Clancy Trial? I Can Certainly Speculate.
2026-09-05

What Was in the Mind of the Holdout Juror in the Lindsay Clancy Trial? I Can Certainly Speculate.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-05

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. On Friday, the judge in the Lindsay Clancy case declared a mistrial after a week of jury deadlock. Clancy is charged with murdering her three children in what prosecutors say was a cold-blooded killing, and in what she says was a fit of psychosis after months spent seeking help and being put on a long list of drugs for her declining mental health. One juror was the holdout, refusing to vote for acquittal. He reportedly recognized that there was reasonable doubt as to her responsibility for her crime, but would not apply the appropriate legal standard. The disarray and unsettledness that characterizes the end of this trial is unsatisfying but also in line with the whole of the Clancy saga. The story is a tragic one: Three innocent children dead, a family in shambles. And it’s also been a galvanizing one, as women the nation over have spoken out in Clancy’s defense, noting their own postpartum struggles; many have shown up at her trial to support her, clad in pink. And there has of course been a backlash, albeit a smaller one, of people who insist that a mother who kills her children must simply be evil, no other explanation necessary (or even possible). It’s hard to know what was in the mind of the lone holdout juror, the one who wanted to find Clancy guilty of being in her right mind when she killed her children even as he realized the prosecution has not proven its case. I can certainly speculate: Some people want a simple story of good and evil. Some people need a punitive outcome to a horrific event, no matter actual fault or responsibility. Some people just want women punished. Anecdotally, I’ve noticed a sharp gender divide in opinion on the Clancy case (and something of a political divide, too, though a far less pronounced one). The people demanding Clancy go to prison are overwhelmingly conservative men. Women, and especially mothers, are not cheering Clancy on, but are largely calling for some nuance and care. Not every mother struggles in the postpartum period. But many of us—most of us?—have experienced the brain-and-body altering effects of pregnancy, childbirth, and childrearing: Have felt our post-birth hormones crash, sending us reeling into despair; have felt bobble-headed and nearly insane after weeks or months of sleepless, cry-pierced nights, pulling from some reservoir of human survival we didn’t know existed within us to meet seemingly endless needs; have been pushed into unhinged rage and then profound shame by a defiant toddler. And then if you have more than one child, you may well be managing that defiant toddler while simultaneously experiencing the hormone crash, the sleeplessness, the bobble-headed feeling of the newborn period all over again. Most women are not saying “I understand why Lindsay Clancy killed her kids.” But I suspect that most of us know we don’t understand only by the grace of God or luck. Motherhood has taken many of us to new emotional lands—sometimes soaring heights, but sometimes far down into dark depths. Postpartum psychosis, as Clancy says she experienced, is rare. But stunningly high numbers of women report having intrusive thoughts about harming their children—moments when their own brains betray them, when it’s clear that none of us are in total control over our own minds. According to one study, a new mom is just as likely to have disturbing thoughts of intentionally harming her child as she is to not have such thoughts. The women who have these thoughts are not bad or evil. They might, in fact, be excellent, caring mothers. But depending on the severity of the thoughts and their regularity, they may need help—which in the US, they might have a very hard time getting. Much has been written about the women who support Clancy, including those who have descended into conspiracy theorizing. Less attention has been paid to those who are convinced that a suburban mom who was by all accounts deeply loving decided one day to kill her three kids, slash her own neck, and then climb out of a window, leaving herself paralyzed. The whole theory seems to be that she was just a selfish bitch who no longer wanted to be a mom. But that doesn’t quite make sense given her nearly-successful suicide. I suspect, instead, that many people—especially people who have not borne children, especially people who put much stock in traditional gender roles, and especially people who take a more simplistic black-and-white worldview—simply cannot wrap their minds around the fact that sometimes, even good mothers can crack up with the most unthinkably awful results. If your worldview separates women out into “good” and “bad,” with good women pursuing the natural and God-given path of marrying and procreating and dedicating their selfless lives to their children, and bad women working against nature to pursue their own interests or not put their children first, then a mother like Lindsay Clancy is especially destabilizing. She was squarely on the “good woman” side, and then she committed the worst imaginable crime. She always aspired to have lots of kids, her own mother testified, and even her career, as a labor and delivery nurse, was centered around bringing children into the world. The only explanation for the horrific turn her life took must be that she was secretly evil all along. To believe that even good women might see their minds betray them so spectacularly would be to rescind control over an entire ideology that helps organize your world. There are also people who I suspect simply get stuck in the impossible space of wanting justice for three innocent children and finding no clear path to get it. “Justice,” in the parlance of the American legal system and true crime podcasts, generally means punishment, which generally means prison. How might justice be done for Cora, Dawson, and Callan if the person who killed them doesn’t spend the rest of her life behind bars? That she may have committed the crime but may not be actually responsible for it leaves an uncomfortable vacuum, with no sense of finality. The realities of our legal system do not make this any easier. The jury in the Clancy case could have found her guilty and sent her to prison for the rest of her life, which may feel like a fair result for a person who killed three children but is a profoundly unsatisfying and unjust one if the person in question was in the throes of psychosis and not legally responsible for her actions. The jury could have acquited her by reason of insanity, which would then send her to a mental institution, the duration of her stay determined by doctors. That, too, may feel inadequate and potentially perilous—would Clancy be freed in just a few years? Could she go on to have other children? Compassion offers little sense of closure. We don’t know what was motivating the solo juror who wanted to send Clancy to jail rather than to a psychiatric hospital. We don’t know if the prosecution will re-try Clancy, or what will happen next. We do know that scores of mothers saw Lindsay Clancy and felt a twinge of understanding. We know that some of those mothers are still howling for help. We know there are scores of others, mostly men, who seem invested in writing those women off as crazy or unnatural or evil. We know this trial may have “raised awareness” about postpartum mental health, but has not actually changed any tangible aspect of support for women struggling with it. Ultimately, what this trial has revealed is just as incomplete and unsatisfying as its conclusion. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Anyway, enough with the business talk. Let’s chat about something fun, like American politics, and its most important feature: money. MAGA Inc. is the president’s super PAC. As of July 31, it had $403 million on hand. The Trump associates who run it have kept telling and telling and telling House and Senate members that the balance will be spent on their races this fall, a cash bomb to help save GOP majorities in Congress. But it is now September of an election year, and the super PAC has thrown only a few bucks here and there. Where’s the cash? The Atlantic reported last week that Donald Trump was planning to spend soon, but with a twist: He had ordered “a series of television advertisements about his own record that will serve as a backdrop to the hundreds of individual House and Senate contests.” In other words, he called for ads celebrating himself. As you can guess, given Trump’s yawning unpopularity, this idea is going over poorly with the traditional GOP consultant class. Beyond that, will MAGA Inc. unload in individual races, mentioning—or attacking—individual candidates? Given that it is, again, September, Republicans are starting to doubt they’ll see much of this money. As the Washington Post reported, GOP leaders have begun to “conclude that it’s too late to fully deploy the cash in the midterms and that he intends to hold on to much of it for his own purposes.” This, dear reader. This is … it’s perfect, isn’t it? The single candidate most in need of MAGA Inc.’s largesse is Texas Senate candidate Ken Paxton. Given all of his scandals, baggage, and poor personal fundraising abilities, Paxton could use a heavy infusion of cash to defeat Democratic candidate James Talarico in a large, expensive state with umpteen media markets. But ever since Trump endorsed Paxton over Sen. John Cornyn (who had a much easier, and cheaper, path to victory) in the primary, Senate Republicans and big Texas donors have been of the mind that it’s Trump’s responsibility—not theirs—to fund his campaign. And so we have a bit of a standoff here. A Texas-size standoff. (Sorry, couldn’t resist. Last week of Surge 1.0!) The campaign to push MAGA Inc. to get involved there showed signs of urgency this week. Senate Majority Leader John Thune said on Fox News that “we’re going to need the president and his team” to get involved. Louisiana Sen. John Kennedy said he was “hoping that the president’s going to spend $100 or $200 million in Texas.” And the dean of Texas strategists, Karl Rove, warned that if Trump doesn’t get involved soon, “Talarico is going to run the message of ‘My opponent is the most corrupt politician in Texas history’ all the way to the victory lane.” All well and good. But have any of these complainers considered some of the nice golden trims Trump could use for his presidential library if he keeps this money? Let’s get priorities straight here. Here’s when we’ll know that artificial intelligence has achieved superintelligence: when we tell ChatGPT “Come up with a political event that the Surge could not possibly look forward to less” and it responds “The Republican midterm convention in Dallas.” (So far, the cowardly robot refuses to “invent one.”) Alas, the convention is happening next week for two days, and our dim view of the whole shebang appears to be widely shared within the GOP. Politico surveyed 70 GOP candidates and members and found that most of them weren’t going because they had convenient scheduling conflicts. As for the programming, the GOP intends to highlight its work on tax cuts and border security, as well as its support for things like stock trading bans for members of Congress. (There is no apparent programming for anti-corruption measures within the West Wing.) Now, there is a world where this could be productive for Republicans if they stuck to the script. But Trump, who was at first scheduled to speak only the second night, has now decided that he will speak both nights, ensuring that his showmanship will dominate all coverage. The good news for the GOP, at least, is that Trump’s speeches on both nights will be drowned out by the first two NFL games of the season. In the Massachusetts Senate primary on Tuesday, 80-year-old Sen. Ed Markey, who was first sent to Washington the year Jimmy Carter won the presidency, handily defeated a primary challenge from Rep. Seth Moulton. Our colleague Ian Prasad Philbrick wrote an excellent breakdown of the result this week. In a state like Massachusetts, where the Democratic campaign strategy is simple—run your ass to the left and never look back—Markey had the progressive bona fides to survive Democratic primary voters’ War on Old Incumbents. Moulton, meanwhile, had coded himself as unforgivably centrist, if not viciously right-wing, by saying, after the 2024 election, that he didn’t believe that trans women should play in women’s sports. This marks the second consecutive time Old Man Markey has fended off a former rising star in the party, having tanked Joe Kennedy III’s challenge in 2020. We would find it unusual if Markey ran again in 2032. If he does, though, good luck to whichever next-big-thing in Massachusetts politics chooses to end their career by primarying him. Two House Democrats—Washington Rep. Marie Gluesenkamp Perez and Maine Rep. Jared Golden—broke ranks with their party this week on a procedural vote teeing up votes on several bills, allowing the procedural vote to pass. The reason they did so was to ensure they got a vote on a bill that would help lobstermen. But their procedural votes also set up a vote on a messaging bill from Republicans that both condemned socialism “in all its forms” (lol) and called for the enactment of the SAVE America Act, Trump and Republicans’ hard-line elections bill. It’s a typical election-year poison-pill resolution that Republicans can use to run campaign ads saying Democrats voted to support socialism. Democratic members in competitive races did not want to vote on it but then had to, thanks to their friends from Washington and Maine. House Democrats were furious, and sass was thrown around freely. House Democratic Leader Hakeem Jeffries said he didn’t get a heads-up from the members, telling reporters, “You’ll have to talk to Katherine”—as in Rep. Katherine Clark, the whip. Rep. Nancy Pelosi, against whom Jeffries will always be measured, said that when she was in charge, “we didn’t even consider that anybody would do that.” Rep. Jim McGovern, the top Democrat on the Rules Committee, turned his back to Gluesenkamp Perez while she testified in the committee the following day. Members are so angry that the party is considering punishments for the members—Golden will be leaving Congress in a hot minute and got his lobster bill passed, so he couldn’t care less—and new caucus rules to ensure that such things don’t happen again. That might be a little heavy-handed when a simple talking-to, or a vote-whipping operation that’s more on top of things, could suffice. Last week, we wrote about Pete Hegseth’s firing of Gen. Randy George, the Army chief of staff, over a repeated series of clashes, including the question of whether a military helicopter fly-by at Kid Rock’s house was worthy of investigation. This week, Army Secretary Dan Driscoll submitted his resignation, a move that “had been seen as increasingly imminent after months of friction with Hegseth and others aligned with the Pentagon chief,” per the Post. What’s interesting about this one is that Driscoll has friends in places above Hegseth’s pay grade. He’s friends with Vice President J.D. Vance, with whom he attended Yale Law School. And when Driscoll personally brought his concerns about Hegseth’s leadership to Trump last week, Trump both encouraged Driscoll to stay on and reportedly “expressed concern about the deep cuts to the Army’s senior leadership.” Could this be the beginning of Hegseth losing favor with Trump? We’re going to say … no, Trump probably forgot about it in five minutes, when he resumed thinking about marble samples for the ballroom. Good news: These stupid midterms will be over soon, and then we can turn to the stupid presidential race for our yuks. On the GOP side, don’t expect it to simply be a race between Secretary of State Marco Rubio and Vance. Among others, Sen. Ted Cruz, who has waited three long cycles to give it another go after placing second to Trump in 2016, is making moves. As the Wall Street Journal reported from Iowa this week, Cruz has been “quietly asking people if they would be interested in working on his 2028 bid.” For some time now, too, he’s been pushing back on elements of MAGA, such as the growing antisemitism in the Tucker Carlson wing of the far right and overreaches by the Federal Communications Commission. In other words, he’s trying to carve a lane for himself as the representative of pre-Trump conservatism. Such a strategy depends on there being a backlash within the GOP to Trump-style populism, as well as general sloppiness as his presidency wanes. All we can say is, uh, good luck, we guess? The main reason we’re posting this is because Vance was asked about a Cruz 2028 run on Thursday and had, essentially, the same response as us: “And on Ted Cruz, I mean, look, man, he’s just clearly—Ted is a very authentic and likable guy, and I’m sure he’ll do great.” A deluge of cattiness awaits us in 2027. It’s past time to say it: This Supreme Court is helping President Donald J. Trump evade accountability. Erwin Chemerinsky, dean of the Berkeley School of Law, joins Dahlia Lithwick to break down the conservative majority’s procedural toolkit—standing, ripeness, the Purcell principle, and inscrutable rulings on the shadow docket—and how these doctrinal headfakes are being used to ensure Trump always wins in court, even when he loses. Can You Really Get Fired From Fox News for Being Too Trumpy? I’m Skeptical.
2026-09-04

Can You Really Get Fired From Fox News for Being Too Trumpy? I’m Skeptical.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Maria Bartiromo, the conservative TV host known for her staunch support of Donald Trump, is out at Fox News. Generally speaking, we at the Slatest try to avoid getting wrapped up in palace intrigue, especially media palace intrigue. But this particular ouster offers an important window into how things really work at Fox—as well as into the broader conservative media ecosystem in which the network is the apex predator. Details are still trickling out, but here’s what we know. On Thursday, Fox announced that Bartiromo was “no longer with” the network, effective immediately. It didn’t give a reason, but reporters quickly put the pieces together. The drama seems to have started in July, when a Fox executive told the network’s on-air personalities to shy away from talking about a major Trump speech about election security. In that primetime address, the president floated conspiratorial insinuations about other countries interfering in the 2020 election, which he claims was stolen from him. Bartiromo, who has covered Trump’s election lies before, apparently forwarded the Fox executive’s guidance to the White House—“as if to say it wasn’t her fault that the claims weren’t getting airtime,” CNN reports—and Fox reportedly labeled Bartiromo’s disclosure a violation of her employment agreement. She’s contesting the emerging narrative—and maybe her apparent ouster, too. “The irresponsible reports that have been published stating that Maria Bartiromo was fired or is no longer an employee of Fox are absolutely and unequivocally false,” Bartiromo’s lawyer said today. “Make no mistake, we have the receipts and witnesses and they will come out, whether through the courthouse or otherwise.” Spicy! But assuming the reporting is right, is Bartiromo really the first Fox employee to get too close to the White House? No. During his first term, the president reportedly consulted the network’s on-air personalities for advice, sometimes even putting them on speakerphone in the Oval Office. One regular confidant was Sean Hannity, who even appeared briefly at a campaign rally with the president. Yet Hannity remains a Fox News host in good standing, and the network’s airwaves have become even more of a revolving door since Trump retook power. Several top officials this term—including Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth and Jeanine Pirro, the U.S. attorney for the District of Columbia—were previously Fox personalities. Nor does Bartiromo seem like the only Fox insider who’s passed along sensitive internal info. Roger Ailes, the network’s former CEO, reportedly tipped off Trump’s 2016 campaign about a question later posed by a Fox reporter, and Hannity even hid the fact that he’d apparently shared a lawyer with the president (Michael Cohen, who’s now back in Trump’s good graces). Hang on: Fox (reportedly) fired Bartiromo! So doesn’t that prove the network isn’t just an arm of the White House? Trump’s 2020 election lies are among his most consistently expressed beliefs. Declining to give them airtime—and cutting ties with a host who has promoted them—certainly looks like an act of courage. But it has to be considered in the context of a network that constantly promotes many of Trump’s other lies. And, as noted, network personnel have been caught colluding with him. So what’s different here? There may be a baser motive at play: money. Bartiromo’s zealous coverage of the president’s claims had become a legal headache, helping cost Fox hundreds of millions of dollars in one defamation suit. Oh yeah. Remind me what happened in that case? In 2021, Dominion Voting Systems sued Fox for airing unsubstantiated allegations—including on Bartiromo’s show—that the company’s ballot-tabulation machines were rigged to steal the election. Just as the case was about to go to trial, Fox settled for $787 million. Fox is also facing a similar suit from another voting machine company, Smartmatic, in which Bartiromo is a defendant. According to NPR, Fox’s lawyers went to court for a hearing in that case on Thursday—the same day the network announced Bartiromo’s departure. (Bartiromo’s wouldn’t be the first Trump-friendly head to roll over the president’s election lies. The network ousted longtime host Lou Dobbs after Smartmatic sued and parted ways with Tucker Carlson, who also aired Trump’s false claims, days after the Dominion settlement.) Ouch. But what does this episode tell us about conservative media in general? It leads to some pretty cynical conclusions about the Murdochs, the billionaire family that owns Fox News, the New York Post, and other organs of the right-wing information ecosystem. Rupert Murdoch, who officially handed off his media empire to his son Lachlan last year, arguably helped create the political substrate that germinated Trump, who regularly appeared on Fox to criticize Barack Obama years before running for president. The Trump era has also been good for the Murdochs’ properties. Fox remains the country’s most-watched cable news network, and its other hosts regularly carry water for Trump’s agenda. So rather than taking a stand for the truth or against the president, Fox seems to have ditched Bartiromo to save face—and maybe cash. Trump isn’t pleased; “her fans, of which there are many, will not be happy,” he wrote in a valedictory Truth Social post. Yet Bartiromo’s ouster seems unlikely to break the symbiotic relationship that continues to benefit both the president and his favorite network. In response to questions about why she was let go, Fox called it “a business decision.” Indeed. It’s the holiday weekend, so you, like Bartiromo, likely have some extra free time on your hands. My colleagues have some great suggestions for how to spend it. Go to the movies: If you’re planning a trip to the theater this weekend, allow us to offer some suggestions. If you want to watch a comedy, check out Coyote vs. Acme. If you’re looking for action and adventure, Sam calls The End of Oak Street a “nasty little throwback to Jurassic Park.” For horror fans, It Ends will shake you to your core. If you’re in the mood for a coming-of-age drama, the Anthony Bourdain biopic Tony is “is a perfect little movie,” according to Dana Stevens. We would recommend skipping The Dog Stars and The Whisper Man, which were both disappointing adaptations of great books. Binge a new TV show: If you’d rather stay in and stream something, we have a couple of recommendations. Sterling Point is a summer romance series that “will stand the test of time,” according to Rebecca Onion. Rebecca also raves about the crime thriller Furious, which features one of the best performances on TV right now. Lanterns, writes Sam Adams, “is a superhero show for people who think they don’t like superhero shows.” Sam was also pleasantly surprised by the newest season of Ted Lasso, which bounced back after its previous dreadful season. Finally, if you are craving some juicy reality TV, Scaachi Koul dubbed TLC’s Double Lives of Suburban Wives, which follows a group of Christian moms doing sex work, an “absolute banger.” Celebrate Labor Day with a new tradition: Sorry to Bother You, Boots Riley’s 2018 film starring LaKeith Stanfield, is a rollicking sci-fi black comedy about a dystopian workplace. It is also, argues Terri Gerstein, the perfect movie to rewatch this weekend. She argues that what It’s a Wonderful Life is to Christmas and Groundhog Day is to Groundhog Day, Sorry to Bother You should be to Labor Day. Sample some new snacks: Summer barbecues are rife with potato-chip mediocrity. This weekend, leave the predictable bag of chips behind and win the cookout with thrilling bites from Thailand, Canada, and even … Pennsylvania. Danny Palumbo recommends the best snacks to enliven your cookout. Or, if you prefer softer spuds, Danny also has a genius tip for leveling up your potato salad. As the last thing standing between you and a long weekend, let me get out of your way. We’re taking Labor Day off, so I’ll be back in your inbox on Tuesday. ’Til then! The Lindsay Clancy Case Has Ended In a Mistrial. What Happens Now?
2026-09-04

The Lindsay Clancy Case Has Ended In a Mistrial. What Happens Now?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. The case of Lindsay Clancy has officially ended in a mistrial. The high-profile murder case has consumed national media—for better and for worse—for weeks now, as a jury considered whether the Massachusetts mother intentionally took the lives of her three children or if her crime (and punishment) could be mitigated by reason of insanity. After the jury spent nearly 40 hours deliberating, the judge declared a mistrial on Friday afternoon. That means this case is far from over, with plenty of options, and questions, remaining about Clancy’s fate—and if and how the commonwealth of Massachusetts will try her again. For some broader context: Clancy’s case was exceptionally rare, as she pleaded guilty to strangling her three children back in 2023 but argued that she should not be held criminally liable because she was suffering from postpartum psychosis at the time. The prosecution argued that she was able to parse right from wrong and preplanned to commit the atrocities at the Clancy family home. The jury, made up of nine women and three men, spent six days deliberating the case and twice sent notes to the judge indicating they could not reach a unanimous verdict. Kevin Reddington, Clancy’s defense attorney, suggested that a sole juror had admitted to feeling there was reasonable doubt concerning her guilt but refused to join the 11 jurors who were ready to find Clancy not guilty, per her insanity defense. On Friday, as Judge William Sullivan was announcing a mistrial, Reddington asked to appeal his decision with the Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court. Sullivan obliged, allowing Reddington one hour to file his appeal, but it ultimately failed, with the state Supreme Court quickly holding a hearing and denying the application for an emergency stay. To unpack the other complicated details of this case’s non-ending, I spoke with Effie Blassberger, a trial attorney at Clayman Rosenberg Kirshner & Linder who focuses on #MeToo–related litigation alongside white-collar criminal defense. Here is our conversation, which has been edited and condensed for clarity. Shirin Ali: The jury’s landing point in this case was described by Clancy’s attorney as an “intractable position.” What does that mean, specifically within the context of reasonable doubt? Effie Blassberger: It’s basically saying that one juror has a fixed view and it’s not moving. The judge sent the jury back to deliberate several times, and the idea behind juror deliberations is you’re supposed to discuss the evidence with one another with candor, to openly listen. But it sounds like there was one juror whose position is intractable, that they have this fixed view that isn’t changing, no matter how much more deliberations continue. When it comes to reasonable doubt, if a juror actually believes there’s reasonable doubt, that’s supposed to work in Clancy’s favor. That’s the whole point of the standard. Reasonable doubt isn’t something you just note and move past. It’s supposed to end the jury’s deliberations in the defendant’s favor. Reddington suggested that the holdout juror did acknowledge doubt but still wanted to convict anyway. That’s his whole argument, that this one juror wasn’t following the law as instructed and he wanted to have him removed. But that being said, you have to keep in mind that we are getting this information secondhand—it’s a juror note that’s being interpreted and characterized by Reddington. This is not a note from the holdout juror. It’s coming from the point of view of jurors who are frustrated with this lone juror who is not agreeing with them and their characterization of this juror. How unusual is it for there to be one holdout juror that ends up overtaking a jury’s decisionmaking like this? I think it’s less unusual than people think. Hung juries do happen across criminal trials; obviously, they are more rare and infrequent. However, when a jury hangs, it’s usually in this exact sort of situation, where it’s not an even split—it’s one person who won’t move. It is also common and happens throughout every jury trial that jurors send notes. That’s how they communicate with the judge and the lawyers. Some of those notes might ask that they see certain evidence again, they might want to see certain transcripts, they might need the judge to instruct them on the law. Sometimes, through questions that jurors are asking, both the judge and the lawyers might gain some insights into what the jurors are thinking in a particular case, and it certainly seems that that’s what happened here. My understanding is that Reddington has, through these notes, come to his own assessment that except for this holdout juror, there’s an 11-to-1 split leaning toward not guilty by reason of lack of criminal responsibility. We know that Reddington asked the judge to have this one juror thrown out, but the judge said no. What considerations do judges make in these types of situations? It’s certainly rare that somebody’s trying to remove one juror at this stage of the case, and it’s important to grasp why he’s trying to do that. The only way to do it is by saying: Look, this juror is not following his oath. He’s not able to follow the law. That’s my understanding that was gleaned from one of the juror notes. However, simply because the juror is holding out and not agreeing with other jurors, that’s not a basis to remove them. It’s a very delicate balance here because this whole system works by respecting the integrity of the deliberations and the process. If this one juror just had strong convictions that happen to be different from everyone else’s, the law allows that, and it’s not for a judge or anyone else to intervene after the jury has been instructed to try to come to a consensus. The judge’s only role here is to make sure the jurors are following the law, and unless he had reason to believe that this juror was not following and applying the law, he was not going to intervene. If it was so clear-cut that the juror was actually saying, “I have reasonable doubt, but I want to convict,” the judge’s decision about whether there was good cause to remove the juror would have been much more straightforward. But that’s clearly not what happened here. I don’t think it was as simple as Reddington, perhaps, is characterizing the note, or as the media is characterizing the note. Remember, Reddington seemed to think that but for this lone juror, the verdict would have been in his client’s favor. He went down the only avenue that was available to him after he made his motion to appeal to the judge, and that motion was denied. My understanding is that it had a very low chance of success, but as a defense attorney you have to do everything you can for your client and to exhaust every possible avenue, and he did that. Reddington’s appeal failed, and Sullivan officially declared a mistrial. What might the prosecution do next? There are three options. They can retry the case with a brand-new jury. Or they could negotiate some sort of plea deal with Clancy. My understanding is that there might be limitations on what pleas are available based on the counts that are in this case. The last option would be to not pursue this case further. My understanding is that the district attorney’s office hasn’t said anything publicly yet about how they intend to pursue. My sense is, given how much public attention this case has gotten, they are going to retry her. It’s hard to envision that the prosecutors learned anything different about this trial. Presumably these were the choices that they had before, they could have tried her or offered a plea deal, and I don’t think the trial produced any new information that the prosecution learned that changed their view of this case. They believe that this was intentional and premeditated, and I think they’re going to stand by those beliefs. I don’t think the public attention is going to sway the prosecution, but finding a new jury who is impartial is going to be really challenging, because this case has received so much media attention. I think in the future, attorneys would try to screen this out during jury selection. You can ask questions to try to screen this out, and it’s worth pointing out that this is not the first time—there are cases that have received so much media attention. Those cases do get tried, but it’s certainly challenging. The DA presumably makes the final decision to retry Clancy. He’ll likely consult with others in his office, but ultimately it’s his decision to make. And he would try the same exact charges but have a new jury. She has been denied bail, so she’s been held in custody since 2023 at the Tewksbury State Hospital. Now that a mistrial has been declared, that does not equate to an acquittal. Nothing changes, at least immediately, in terms of where she stands; she’ll continue staying at Tewksbury. Her lawyer might try to have her bail reconsidered, but the fact that there’s been a mistrial doesn’t really change Clancy’s circumstances. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. If you want to block ads but still support Slate, consider subscribing. Already a member? Sign in here

Trump’s Attack on the Postal Service Echoes Another President’s Shameful Plot
2026-09-04

Trump’s Attack on the Postal Service Echoes Another President’s Shameful Plot

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. President Donald Trump’s executive order directing the post office to suppress mail voting has often been called “unprecedented.” That is not quite right. An earlier president—in fact, one of Trump’s role models—also empowered the Postal Service to squelch political opposition. It was one of the most disreputable episodes in U.S. history, and Trump has attempted to revive it with a modern twist. In the summer of 1835, the American Anti-Slavery Society began mailing a pamphlet titled Human Rights, along with other literature, to thousands of civic and religious leaders in Southern states, hoping to convince readers of the immorality of slavery. Things did not go as planned. That July, a shipment that included Human Rights, the Emancipator, and a children’s magazine called the Slave’s Friend arrived in Charleston, South Carolina. The local postmaster, a Jacksonian Democrat named Alfred Huger, was unnerved by what he considered inflammatory content and sequestered the abolitionist material, rather than comply with his legal obligation to deliver the mail. That evening, a mob of so-called lynch men broke into the warehouse and removed the mailbags, which they soon consigned to a great bonfire that attracted thousands of cheering spectators. Huger wrote for instructions to Postmaster General Amos Kendall, as well as New York Postmaster Samuel Gouverneur, from whose office the offending literature had been shipped. Gouverneur agreed to refuse acceptance of abolitionist shipments pending a ruling from Kendall. Kendall had been a key backer of Democrat Andrew Jackson in the 1828 election, having defected from Henry Clay’s Whigs. His reward was appointment to Jackson’s “Kitchen Cabinet,” in which he was widely considered one of the president’s most loyal advisers. After his reelection in 1832, Jackson nominated Kendall to be postmaster general. The appointment was controversial, as Jackson’s opponents feared the consequences of putting such a partisan figure in charge of the nation’s communications. Nonetheless, Kendall was confirmed. He did not let Jackson down. Kendall’s reply to the Charleston postmaster was careful but unmistakable. Acknowledging that he lacked statutory authority to ban material from the mail, he nonetheless ruled against delivery of the abolitionist periodicals. “By no act, or direction of mine, official or private, could I be induced to aid, knowingly, in giving circulation to papers of this description, directly or indirectly,” he explained. Jackson, a slaveholder and the brutal enforcer of Indian removal, enthusiastically agreed that the “wicked plan of exciting the negroes to insurrection and to massacre” had to be stopped. He instructed Southern postmasters to record and publicize the names of even individual subscribers to abolitionist newspapers to encourage intimidation by their neighbors and perhaps even compel them to leave the South. In his message to Congress that year, Jackson recommended legislation to prohibit, “under severe penalties, the circulation in the Southern States, through the mail, of incendiary publications intended to instigate the slaves to insurrection,” which of course meant any abolitionist pamphlets or newspapers. Jackson’s proposal died in the Senate, but that did not end postal censorship of abolitionist periodicals. Many Southern states passed their own laws and resolutions mandating rejection of abolitionist material, on the spurious claim that it tended “to excite the slaves of the southern states to insurrection and revolt.” Southern postmasters thus continued to withhold antislavery periodicals, with the tacit approval of Jackson protégé Kendall. The local suppression of abolitionist mail persisted throughout the antebellum era during the administrations of Jackson’s eight successors, none of whom enforced the federal statute prohibiting detention of the mail. In 1857, for example, Attorney General Caleb Cushing, appointed by President James Buchanan, opined that Mississippi law superseded the federal postal statute because of the state’s right, under the Constitution, to protect itself “against ‘domestic violence’ and to make provisions to ‘suppress insurrection.’ ” Consequently, the postmaster in Yazoo City, Mississippi, could not be required to deliver printed matter that tended “to produce disaffection, disorder and rebellion among the colored population of the state.” Both Jackson’s and Trump’s efforts to manipulate the mail have rested on fabricated premises. In Jackson’s case, it was the racist myth that enslaved people were content with their condition and would remain passive unless instigated by Northern abolitionists. In Trump’s case, it is the myth that mail ballots—the same method Trump uses to vote—are a form of election rigging. Or, as he put it with characteristic mendacity and capitalization, “ELECTIONS CAN NEVER BE HONEST WITH MAIL IN BALLOTS.” The slavocrats’ abuse of the Postal Service ended only with the Civil War. The Supreme Court has the power to terminate Trump’s voter-suppression scheme much sooner. We shall see. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. BepiColombo sheds its ride and starts the final glide to Mercury
2026-09-05

BepiColombo sheds its ride and starts the final glide to Mercury

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-05

Visit Original Website
Eight year after launch, Mercury probe and its Mercury Transfer Module consciously uncouple as orbit approaches After nearly eight years, ESA and JAXA's BepiColombo spacecraft is finally nearing Mercury, as engineers begin the arrival sequence by jettisoning the power module that got it this far. The spacecraft stack separated from the Mercury Transfer Module (MTM) on September 3 at around 1400 CEST. After a tense few minutes, controllers confirmed that a preliminary Doppler signal had been detected, indicating that the MTM had separated successfully; by 1552 CEST, it was confirmed that signals had been picked up, indicating that all had gone to plan. The solar panels of the Mercury Planetary Orbiter (MPO) were charging the spacecraft's batteries. The next stage involves dropping into orbit around Mercury on November 21. Then the Mercury Magnetospheric Orbiter (MMO) – aka Mio – will be released between December 9 and 10, and a week later MPO will start moving to its final orbit over 480 x 1500 km, with science commencing in April 2027. The arrival is a year later than planned. BepiColombo was supposed to have been orbiting Mercury in December 2025, but problems with the spacecraft's Solar Electric Power (SEP) system meant the trajectory had to be rethought, which extended the spacecraft's cruise. BepiColombo is no stranger to Mercury and has performed six flybys of the planet since 2021. It has also conducted flybys of Venus and Earth following its 2018 launch. The spacecraft is an impressive example of international cooperation. The MPO and MTM are ESA contributions, while the MMO comes from JAXA. ESA also provided the sunshield to protect the MMO during transit. This sunshield will be ejected after the MMO is released. The MPO is the big brother of the BepiColombo partnership, with a main body measuring 2.4 x 2.2 x 1.7 m and a mass of 1,230 kg (including an 85 kg science payload). JAXA's MMO is 1.8 m in diameter and 1.1 m high, and weighs 255 kg (45 kg of which is the science payload). While MPO is 3-axis stabilized and nadir-pointing, MMO will be spin-stabilized at 15 rpm, with its spin axis 90 degrees to the Sun. The goal of the mission is a study of Mercury, "from the structure and dynamics of its magnetosphere and how it interacts with the solar wind, to the properties of its large iron core and the origin of its magnetic field." The initial mission at Mercury is planned for one Earth year (approximately four Mercury years), although it could be extended by another Earth year. ® Eight year after launch, Mercury probe and its Mercury Transfer Module consciously uncouple as orbit approaches Now comes the harder sell: Convincing customers and merchants to trust AI with purchases AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk Hacking education to thrive in the AI world is the first and best step in keeping control Phishers find a new use for invisible Unicode tag characters AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk $12.9 billion deal will inevitably cement Nvidia's market dominance and harm competition in the process. Anthropic lays groundwork for bots that shop for you
2026-09-04

Anthropic lays groundwork for bots that shop for you

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Now comes the harder sell: Convincing customers and merchants to trust AI with purchases Anthropic has blueprints for building e-commerce bots that shop on behalf of clients. That's not something many consumers want at the moment, but the AI biz this week published a set of templates for Claude-based shopping agents and merchant agents in the hope of encouraging more e-commerce businesses to implement automated purchasing. Its blueprint "contains the harnesses, patterns, and guardrails an engineering team needs to get a commerce agent running in days, with reference implementations of a shopping agent and a merchant agent for retail, travel, telecom, and ticketing platforms," the company said. Large retailers and ecommerce platforms, Anthropic observes, are using Claude to build AI agents to simplify online shopping by allowing customers to pose natural language queries to find, compare, and buy items. The buying bit may be wishful thinking: A recent Gartner survey found that while consumers use AI to find products and conduct research, they're not ready to delegate purchasing authority to AI agents. The survey found just 11 percent of consumers are willing to let AI make purchase decisions. A recent Accenture survey [PDF] is a bit more optimistic, noting, "Three in four (74 percent) consumers would let an AI agent handle routine tasks; one in three (32 percent) are ready to hand over purchase decisions; and 9 percent are open to fully autonomous shopping, before the technology is fully operational." Either way, at least AI-assisted product finding and comparison appear to be plausible activities. Anthropic's GitHub repo contains functional shopping and merchant agents that can be built with the Messages API, Agent SDK, or Claude Managed Agents. The biz's shopping agent blueprint comes with ways to connect to product catalogs, online shopping carts, checkout systems, preference databases, and purchase history databases. "A customer can say 'I need a tent, sleeping bag, and stove for a weekend trip with two kids,' and the agent can take it from there," Anthropic suggests, noting that its code supports guardrails "designed to constrain prices and products to actual catalog data, and avoids manipulative upsell patterns." Why might prices need constraint? Well, beyond the possibility of hallucinated pricing, there's considerable concern about how AI agents have the potential to compound the problems with dynamic pricing – where customers are charged different amounts for the same goods. "The rise of agentic AI, where algorithms monitor online behavior to customize costs, will likely exacerbate dynamic pricing," said the Brookings Institution earlier this year. "These agents can assess individual behavior and tailor prices based on a consumer’s shopping and purchasing history." Concerns about AI surveillance pricing were discussed last month at a Senate Judiciary subcommittee hearing. In prepared remarks, Lindsay Owens, president and CEO of Groundwork Collaborative, a Washington, DC-based advocacy group, noted that already half of those using the Walmart app use "Sparky," the company's AI assistant. Those shoppers spend about 35 percent more than those who don't, Owens said. "These agents have access to swaths of data on customers – what shoppers tell them, what they’ve purchased in the past, and in some cases, what they’ve hovered over with their mouse or even what other websites they’ve visited," she said. "All of that can be used to infer exactly how much they’re willing to pay for a product and set the price tag accordingly." Even if shoppers can be assured of getting a fair deal using AI agents, there's another concern. Monica Eaton, founder and CEO of Chargebacks911, told The Register in an emailed statement that agentic commerce looks likely to create fraud problems for merchants. There isn't yet a framework for merchants to deal with AI purchases that consumers claim they didn't authorize. "Merchants cannot become the insurer of every misunderstanding between a consumer and their AI," she said. Anthropic's templates may prove helpful for AI agent design, but the real challenge isn't technical. It's about trust – consumers have to trust that AI will act in their best interest and merchants have to trust that AI will be accountable. That may take time. ® Eight year after launch, Mercury probe and its Mercury Transfer Module consciously uncouple as orbit approaches Now comes the harder sell: Convincing customers and merchants to trust AI with purchases AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk Hacking education to thrive in the AI world is the first and best step in keeping control Phishers find a new use for invisible Unicode tag characters AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk $12.9 billion deal will inevitably cement Nvidia's market dominance and harm competition in the process. AMD's Threadripper Halo is a local-AI workstation for researchers with deep pockets
2026-09-04

AMD's Threadripper Halo is a local-AI workstation for researchers with deep pockets

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk Machine learning researchers have a new and expensive option for doing local AI. AMD just raided its spare parts bin and cobbled together a DGX Station rival boasting up to 576 GB of HBM3e memory that it’s calling the Threadripper Halo. Announced at IFA 2026, the system, which is set to launch next year, marks the first time that AMD’s Instinct accelerators have been offered in a workstation form factor. But much like the AI Halo we reviewed earlier this year, the Threadripper Halo isn’t packing anything we haven’t seen before. The liquid-cooled system is powered by a 96-core Threadripper PRO 9995WX, which you may recall made its debut in 2025. The CPU is backed by up to 2 TB of DDR5 memory and up to 2.6 TB of combined system memory. As you probably already guessed, AMD is positioning the box as a local AI workstation for researchers. From what we gather, the system can be had with up to four of the company’s PCIe-based Instinct GPUs — though the systems displayed at IFA only showed two installed. Launched back in May, the MI350P is essentially half of an MI350X that’s been crammed into a PCIe form factor. Each accelerator packs 144 GB of speedy HBM3e good for 4 TB/s of memory bandwidth, and up to 4.6 petaFLOPS of FP4 compute. But we’ll note that it's in its 600 W configuration. When deployed in the Threadripper Halo, we expect AMD will be running the cards in the more sedate 450 W config. Combined, the Threadripper Halo promises up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth, enabling the system to run models exceeding a trillion parameters in size (at four-bit precision) entirely in GPU memory. By offloading some of the model to system memory, the system should be able to run the largest open weights models, like Moonshot.AI’s 2.8 trillion-parameter Kimi K3. The ability to run large frontier-class models on your desk won’t come cheap and may not be available in every market, at least not without an electrical service upgrade. AMD hasn’t shared pricing, but as configured, we expect it to sell for somewhere between $100,000 and $150,000. A quad MI350P system is going to push the limits of a standard North American power outlet unless AMD either underclocks the cards from 600 W to 300 W or it mandates a 20 amp circuit. This might explain why the system AMD showed off at IFA only had two. At this price and power level, the system is well positioned to compete with Nvidia’s own high-end AI workstation, the DGX Station. Announced at GTC in 2025, the DGX Station features a 252 GB B300 GPU and a 72-core Grace CPU along with 496 GB of LPDDR5x memory, and it retails for around $100,000 if you can find one in stock. The House of Zen boasts that the system offers up to 3.4x the total system memory and more than twice the memory bandwidth of the DGX Station, which should give an edge in LLM inference, assuming tensor parallel operations don’t bottleneck on the CPU’s PCIe bus. AMD says the Threadripper Halo will be available starting next year. ® Eight year after launch, Mercury probe and its Mercury Transfer Module consciously uncouple as orbit approaches Now comes the harder sell: Convincing customers and merchants to trust AI with purchases AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk Hacking education to thrive in the AI world is the first and best step in keeping control Phishers find a new use for invisible Unicode tag characters AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk $12.9 billion deal will inevitably cement Nvidia's market dominance and harm competition in the process.
